I  hope everybody had a restful weekend. So what I want to do is just kind  of tell you, first, I'm going to go through those questions, make sure  everybody was able to follow my instructions. Then I'm going to go over  what today's schedule is and then we'll return to the Commonwealth's  presentation of evidence. All right. And so let me ask those questions.  First question, has any member of the jury read, seen, or overheard  anything from any source about any aspect of this case that would affect  your ability to be fair and impartial?

Court ([00:33](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=33.93)):

[inaudible 00:00:36].

Judge William Sullivan ([00:35](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=35.58)):

All  right. Next question. Is there any other serious matter or concern  bearing on your service as a juror in this case that anybody needs to  bring to my attention? All right. Again, thank you for following those  instructions. Now, today's schedule, let me say first thing about the  schedule. I know I haven't always been completely correct on some of the  schedules we followed, but I want to just let you know that those  decisions about the scheduling is on me. All right? And sometimes I have  to take into consideration other factors or things that are going on.  So what I really wanted to tell you is that don't speculate why we might  go a little late or a little early on any certain days, but certainly  don't hold it against either side in regards to that. The person who's  making those decisions or that decision is me. All right? So you should  be aware of that.

([01:29](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=89.85))
So  that being said, the thought is today, we should go into the afternoon.  All right? And if that changes, I'll let you know. And what we're going  to do at this point, we're going to return to the Commonwealth's case.  And with that, Commonwealth, call your next witness, please.

Good morning.

Judge William Sullivan ([04:05](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=245.64)):

All right.

May I inquire?

Judge William Sullivan ([04:07](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=247.2)):

Attorney Buckingham, please.

Shanan Buckingham ([04:08](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=248.19)):

Thank you. Good morning, sir. Could you please tell the jurors your first and last name?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([04:12](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=252.54)):

Yep. Michael Snyder.

Shanan Buckingham ([04:14](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=254.22)):

Do you mind spelling your last name for the record, please?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([04:16](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=256.29)):

S-N-Y-D-E-R.

Shanan Buckingham ([04:18](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=258.21)):

And how are you employed?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([04:20](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=260.19)):

I'm a physician at Beth Israel.

Shanan Buckingham ([04:23](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=263.22)):

And how long have you been a physician?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([04:26](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=266.31)):

Since 1998.

Shanan Buckingham ([04:28](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=268.26)):

Can you tell us a little bit about your educational background?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([04:30](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=270.99)):

So,  undergrad at UMass Amherst. I did medical school at Tufts University,  and then I did my residency in emergency medicine at Boston University.

Shanan Buckingham ([04:40](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=280.26)):

And how long have you worked at Beth Israel?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([04:44](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=284.94)):

Since 2014, 12 years.

Shanan Buckingham ([04:49](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=289.11)):

And do you primarily work in the emergency department?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([04:52](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=292.05)):

Yes.

Shanan Buckingham ([04:53](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=293.34)):

I'm  going to draw your attention to January 24th of the year 2023. Do you  recall working in the emergency department on that day?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([04:59](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=299.1)):

Yes.

Shanan Buckingham ([04:59](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=299.94)):

And in the evening, were you made aware of multiple pediatric traumas that were coming into the emergency department?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([05:07](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=307.47)):

Yes.

Shanan Buckingham ([05:08](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=308.28)):

And when you are made aware of people coming in, how does that occur?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([05:15](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=315.03)):

So we'll typically get a call on the EMS phone that something's coming and that they want us to know about upfront.

Shanan Buckingham ([05:23](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=323.76)):

And  do they provide you with some basic background information about what's  occurring or what occurred on scene and what's occurring during the  transport?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([05:34](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=334.56)):

When possible, yeah.

Shanan Buckingham ([05:35](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=335.07)):

Okay. And when the emergency personnel, the EMTs or paramedics arrived at Beth Israel, there were three patients, correct?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([05:42](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=342.51)):

Correct.

Shanan Buckingham ([05:43](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=343.14)):

And you were assigned to treat one patient?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([05:46](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=346.59)):

Correct.

Shanan Buckingham ([05:47](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=347.04)):

Do you recall which patient that was?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([05:48](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=348.27)):

Cora.

Shanan Buckingham ([05:48](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=348.66)):

Okay. And prior to EMS arriving, did you learn more about Cora's presentation and what was occurring?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([05:59](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=359.46)):

Yeah.  So we had heard that all three were in cardiac arrest and Cora in  particular, they were still working on doing CPR and doing what we call  pediatric advanced life support. So medications to try and restart the  heart. And they also mentioned that they had used what we call a  defibrillator to shock the heart to try to restart it. And they were  still doing CPR when they were arriving.

Shanan Buckingham ([06:32](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=392.64)):

And is this information important to you as you're assessing a patient coming into the emergency department?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([06:35](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=395.19)):

Yeah,  yeah. It's important to know what things look like in the field, how  long the patient might've been without a pulse or how long they may not  have been breathing for, so we can kind of expect or know what injuries  to look out for, what we can potentially reverse.

Shanan Buckingham ([06:51](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=411.42)):

And were you aware of the age of Cora?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([06:54](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=414.6)):

Yes.

Shanan Buckingham ([06:55](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=415.53)):

And she was five, correct?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([06:56](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=416.88)):

Yes.

Shanan Buckingham ([06:57](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=417.57)):

And as far as the medications that you were aware of, what medications had been provided?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([07:04](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=424.32)):

So prior to coming to us, she had been given epinephrine, which is the primary medication we use in a cardiac arrest.

Shanan Buckingham ([07:12](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=432.09)):

And do you know how they had administered that medicine to her?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([07:16](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=436.65)):

Yeah.  So she had what we call an intraosseous device in her right tibia.  Sometimes it's very difficult to get an IV in so we can go directly into  the bone or the bone marrow.

Shanan Buckingham ([07:29](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=449.34)):

And were you aware of whether she had been intubated en route?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([07:33](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=453.06)):

So she was not intubated en route.

Shanan Buckingham ([07:36](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=456.33)):

When she arrived at the emergency department, were you there to greet the ambulance?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([07:39](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=459.96)):

Yes.

Shanan Buckingham ([07:40](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=460.68)):

And what happens when a patient comes in through the emergency department via ambulance?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([07:46](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=466.98)):

So  when the patient first comes in, we immediately have our team start to  sort of take over care so there's no gap. And then we also get a sign  out from the paramedics and EMTs and any other staff that had been  involved in the case.

Shanan Buckingham ([08:01](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=481.74)):

And do you and your emergency room staff then perform your own initial assessment of the patient?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([08:07](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=487.56)):

Yes.

Shanan Buckingham ([08:07](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=487.89)):

And what do you do for that?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([08:09](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=489.21)):

So  initially, we sort of go through our ABCs, airway, breathing,  circulation. So initially focusing on the primary things such as making  sure we're getting air and oxygen into the patient, keeping the heart  going, so continuing CPR at that point.

Shanan Buckingham ([08:26](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=506.58)):

And as far as Cora went, did you observe that she was breathing?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([08:30](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=510.69)):

So she was not breathing on her own.

Shanan Buckingham ([08:32](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=512.31)):

How about the heart? Did you observe that her heart was beating?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([08:35](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=515.91)):

There was no heartbeat.

Shanan Buckingham ([08:38](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=518.7)):

How about her physical appearance? What observations did you make of her physical appearance?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([08:42](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=522.99)):

So  initially, when she had come in, some of the things we noticed is sort  of a blue tint to the skin, which typically, we can see when there's  less oxygen or lack of oxygen. And then the other things on a quick  survey is there was some areas of bruising on the neck and around the  eyes called petechiae, kind of little bleeding, episodes of bleeding  under the skin, and I believe a small amount of blood coming from the  nose.

Shanan Buckingham ([09:14](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=554.07)):

Were you able to make observations of her hands or other parts of her body?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([09:19](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=559.17)):

So the hands definitely had that, what we call cyanosis or blue hue.

Shanan Buckingham ([09:25](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=565.95)):

Now,  as far as the eyes, you mentioned petechiae around the face and the  eyes as well as the neck. Did you notice anything else about her eyes?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([09:35](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=575.19)):

So the pupils were fixed and enlarged.

Shanan Buckingham ([09:39](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=579.96)):

Why is that significant to you?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([09:41](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=581.52)):

So  oftentimes, when we see it coming in, it can indicate two different  things. Some of the medications like epinephrine can cause the pupils to  dilate, but also if the brain is starting to become damaged or not  functioning, then you start to lose initially some of the signals that  would make the pupils smaller. So they'll tend to start to get enlarged  or dilated if there's brain injury starting or has occurred.

Shanan Buckingham ([10:11](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=611.07)):

Now,  you indicated that when Cora arrived, she was not intubated, meaning  she did not have a breathing tube in. Did you and your team attempt to  intubate her?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([10:19](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=619.71)):

Yes.

Shanan Buckingham ([10:20](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=620.4)):

And did you do that on your own or with the assistance of other parts of the emergency department or the hospital?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([10:28](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=628.98)):

So  we had anesthesia there. After we got the phone calls of multiple  patients coming in, typically we'll have all the help that we could  possibly need there to be able to take care of all the patients. So I  had an anesthesiologist in the room with me who put the breathing tube  in.

Shanan Buckingham ([10:47](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=647.4)):

In  instances where an individual might have suffered some sort of injury  to their neck, does that complicate the insertion of a breathing tube?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([10:55](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=655.14)):

It  can, yeah. Because normally, you might need to move or tilt the head to  get a direct view where you can put a breathing tube in. But when  there's a concern for neck injury, we stabilize it typically with a  collar or manually so you don't have the ability to move the head and  the neck around to try and get a better view.

Shanan Buckingham ([11:15](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=675.75)):

Once a breathing tube was put in, were you and your team able to manually give breath to Cora?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([11:21](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=681.99)):

Yes.

Shanan Buckingham ([11:22](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=682.74)):

Now, did you then perform an assessment, a cardiac assessment of her?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([11:27](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=687.27)):

Yeah.  So in addition to listening to the heart, feeling for a pulse, we'll  also tend to use an ultrasound machine in the room where we can kind of  get a direct view of heart function.

Shanan Buckingham ([11:40](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=700.41)):

And  you were aware that they had defibrillated her twice in the ambulance.  Why is that significant when you're treating a patient that's in cardiac  arrest?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([11:48](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=708.9)):

So  when they defibrillate a patient, it's typically the heart or might be  in a rhythm where it can't pump blood. So it might be sort of out of  sync or quivering, where we may attempt to use electricity to kind of  shock it back into a rhythm.

Shanan Buckingham ([12:06](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=726)):

Once you performed the ultrasound, the cardiac ultrasound, were there any attempts made further to use a defibrillator?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([12:14](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=734.46)):

No.

Shanan Buckingham ([12:17](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=737.79)):

Throughout your course of treatment with her, did you continue with various medications?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([12:21](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=741.75)):

Yes.

Shanan Buckingham ([12:22](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=742.29)):

And was CPR ongoing throughout your entire time with her?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([12:26](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=746.01)):

Yes.

Shanan Buckingham ([12:27](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=747.18)):

Were you consulting with any other colleagues or any other hospitals in regards to the care of Cora?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([12:32](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=752.73)):

Yeah.  So we actually had, in addition to all my colleagues in the emergency  room, we had intensive care physicians from Children's that were on the  phone with us kind of working through any things that may have come up  in the case. Also expecting that they would be receiving the patients  from us.

Shanan Buckingham ([12:52](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=772.5)):

Is your hospital at the Beth Israel equipped to handle this type of pediatric trauma?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([12:57](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=777.96)):

So  we can do the initial stabilization, but then we need to ship the  patients out to either get a specialty care or continued inpatient care.

Shanan Buckingham ([13:07](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=787.68)):

And how long did efforts in treating Cora continue?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([13:12](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=792.63)):

So in the emergency room, roughly 30 minutes.

Shanan Buckingham ([13:17](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=797.46)):

And fair to say that all of the efforts that you made were not successful?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([13:25](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=805.59)):

Correct.

Shanan Buckingham ([13:26](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=806.34)):

And at approximately 19:28 or 7: 28, did you finally declare Cora deceased?

Dr. Michael Snyder ([13:34](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=814.05)):

Yes.

Shanan Buckingham ([13:35](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=815.1)):

Thank you. I have nothing further.

Judge William Sullivan ([13:35](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=815.34)):

Mr. Reddington.

Kevin Reddington ([13:38](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=818.61)):

I have no questions. Thank you, Doctor.

Judge William Sullivan ([13:39](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=819.12)):

Thank you, Doctor.

Dr. Michael Snyder ([13:40](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=820.74)):

Thanks.

Thank you.

Court Officer 2 ([13:47](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=827.76)):

Morning.

Jennifer Sprague ([14:33](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=873.6)):

If I may?

Judge William Sullivan ([14:34](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=874.26)):

Yes, please.

Jennifer Sprague ([14:35](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=875.52)):

Thank you. Good morning. Can you please state and spell your name for the record?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([14:38](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=878.37)):

My name's Dr. Andrew Capraro. Last name C-A-P-R-A-R-O.

Jennifer Sprague ([14:43](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=883.53)):

And your date of birth, please?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([14:45](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=885.06)):

July 9th, 1970.

Jennifer Sprague ([14:47](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=887.16)):

What do you do for a living?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([14:48](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=888.72)):

I'm an attending physician in the emergency department at Boston Children's Hospital.

Jennifer Sprague ([14:52](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=892.35)):

And how long have you done that?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([14:54](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=894.03)):

For the last 26 years.

Jennifer Sprague ([14:55](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=895.83)):

Can you just briefly describe for us the training and education you had to qualify for that role?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([15:01](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=901.53)):

I  went to Providence College undergrad and then went to Brown Medical  School. Subsequently, I went to Connecticut Children's Medical Center  for my pediatric residency, and then did fellowship training in  pediatric emergency medicine at Boston Children's Hospital from 2000 to  2003 and have been an attending there ever since.

Jennifer Sprague ([15:19](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=919.68)):

And what's involved with being an attending at Boston Children's Hospital?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([15:23](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=923.61)):

It means that I am guiding the care and treatment for the patients that I am seeing during my particular shift.

Jennifer Sprague ([15:31](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=931.41)):

And are you assigned to a specific location or area of the hospital?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([15:35](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=935.52)):

I'm  always in the emergency department and the emergency department is  broken up into different zones of which I am sort of the primary  attending for one set of rooms.

Jennifer Sprague ([15:44](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=944.82)):

I want to direct your attention to January 24th, 2023. Were you working that evening?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([15:51](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=951.12)):

I was.

Jennifer Sprague ([15:51](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=951.21)):

And at some point in time, did you learn that a patient named Callan Clancy was going to be transported to your hospital?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([15:57](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=957.27)):

I did.

Jennifer Sprague ([15:59](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=959.13)):

And do you recall where he was being brought from?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([16:01](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=961.08)):

From Beth Israel Plymouth, I believe.

Jennifer Sprague ([16:04](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=964.2)):

And was he being med-flighted to your facility?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([16:06](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=966.27)):

Correct.

Jennifer Sprague ([16:07](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=967.26)):

And were you provided with some information about the patient in order to prepare for his arrival?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([16:12](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=972.57)):

I was.

Jennifer Sprague ([16:13](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=973.2)):

And what did you learn?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([16:15](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=975.03)):

We  have a communication center where facilities that are transferring  patients to us call to let us know that the patient is coming. And so  that comm center had been notified about this patient, who then got in  touch with me as the attending who would be caring for the patient to  let me know what had happened there and the condition of the patient  when he was arriving.

Jennifer Sprague ([16:34](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=994.8)):

And what did you learn about the condition of the patient, Callan Clancy, as he was being flown to your hospital?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([16:40](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1000.86)):

What  I was told was that it was believed that the patient had been  strangled. And when he arrived at Plymouth, he was in cardiac arrest,  that they had intubated him and initiated CPR, and they had gotten  return of spontaneous circulation, and that they were then going to  transfer him to us for further evaluation and care.

Jennifer Sprague ([16:58](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1018.89)):

And at some point in time, did Callan arrive at your hospital?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([17:01](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1021.38)):

That is correct.

Jennifer Sprague ([17:03](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1023)):

And were you able to observe him physically?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([17:04](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1024.92)):

Yes.

Jennifer Sprague ([17:05](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1025.88)):

What observations did you make about his physical condition?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([17:09](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1029.75)):

He  was cold on arrival and he was quite limp, was not making any  purposeful movements on his own. His pupils were fixed and dilated, and  he had ligature marks about his neck with some swelling of the neck.

Jennifer Sprague ([17:24](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1044.48)):

And his pupils being fixed and dilated, is that medically significant for you?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([17:30](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1050.06)):

Yes, it is.

Jennifer Sprague ([17:30](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1050.75)):

And why is that?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([17:32](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1052.25)):

It  typically means that significant injury has happened to the brain, such  that there is brain swelling that has sort of over... What do I want to  say? It has overwhelmed the capacity of the skull, such that the  swelling has made the brain sort of herniate through the base of the  skull.

Jennifer Sprague ([17:52](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1072.74)):

Does that mean the brain's swelling so much it's pushing through the skull?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([17:56](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1076.28)):

Correct.

Jennifer Sprague ([17:57](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1077.57)):

And what observations did you make of his heart rate?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([18:01](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1081.5)):

His  heart was beating and we did have to provide him with an epinephrine to  keep his heart rate and his blood pressure within normal limits, but it  was beating on its own.

Jennifer Sprague ([18:10](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1090.71)):

And how did you know that you had to provide the epinephrine for him to stabilize that heart rate?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([18:14](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1094.61)):

It  was initiated at BI because they had noticed that his blood pressure  was low and you need to have normal blood pressure for the blood to  perfuse your organs and keep them healthy. And so we were looking for  certain parameters to sort of maintain that, which we did on that  epinephrine drip.

Jennifer Sprague ([18:31](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1111.23)):

And when you say BI, you're referring to Beth Israel?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([18:33](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1113.39)):

Yes. I'm sorry about that.

Jennifer Sprague ([18:34](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1114.53)):

That's okay. And so as far as his breathing, was he breathing on his own?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([18:40](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1120.53)):

He  had been intubated at the outside hospital and he was needing a  ventilator to breathe for him. Occasionally, he would have sort of a  breath on his own, we would notice. We were able to see that because  when he attempted to take a breath on his own, he would trigger the  ventilator to continue that breath. But it was rare, for sure he needed  the ventilator to maintain his ventilation.

Jennifer Sprague ([19:03](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1143.09)):

Would it be fair to say that the majority of his breathing was being done by the ventilator?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([19:08](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1148.04)):

Yes, the vast majority of his breathing.

Jennifer Sprague ([19:11](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1151.61)):

Now,  once you have received this information while he's coming in and then  you've made your own physical observations of Callan, are there certain  tests that you have run on him?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([19:23](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1163.85)):

Yeah.  Our job, once we received him, was to perform further evaluation and  stabilization to help determine what the next steps of his medical care  would be. So prior to him arriving, we had contacted our trauma team and  our ICU doctors to let them know that he was coming and that we would  need their assistance. And then under my care, we performed some routine  blood work, a head CT, a neck CT, and a chest X-ray to sort of help us  guide the next steps.

Jennifer Sprague ([19:53](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1193.31)):

And from the head CT and the other testing that you did, what were you able to learn from the results of those tests?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([19:58](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1198.83)):

The  head CT in particular showed significant brain swelling consistent with  hypoxic-ischemic injury, which was the most significant finding that we  found.

Jennifer Sprague ([20:09](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1209.45)):

So could you please explain for us what is hypoxic-ischemic injury?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([20:14](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1214.28)):

Hypoxic  is just a fancy word for not getting enough oxygen and ischemic is just  a fancy word for not getting enough blood flow. And so when your organs  don't get enough oxygen and blood, they sort of suffer injury and  therefore get to swelling. And so his brain was showing evidence of  that.

Jennifer Sprague ([20:34](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1234.95)):

So  would it be fair to say that his brain wasn't getting enough oxygen and  blood flow and then because of that, it was damaged and swelling?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([20:43](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1243.59)):

Correct.

Jennifer Sprague ([20:46](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1246.41)):

Now, this type of swelling and injury as you're describing it, can that be caused by strangulation, the injury of the brain?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([20:53](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1253.94)):

Most certainly.

Jennifer Sprague ([20:55](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1255.32)):

And you mentioned earlier that you did see some marks around his neck. Is that correct?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([21:01](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1261.77)):

Correct.

Jennifer Sprague ([21:02](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1262.25)):

And what were those consistent with?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([21:04](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1264.38)):

Strangulation of some sort, most likely from an object based upon the linear aspect of the bruising.

Jennifer Sprague ([21:12](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1272.18)):

And when you say the linear aspect of the bruising, does that mean it's in a straight line?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([21:16](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1276.2)):

Correct.

Jennifer Sprague ([21:19](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1279.29)):

Now, once you had the results of this testing, did you make a decision on what to do next?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([21:29](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1289.64)):

Yeah.  The main thing was to help us figure out did the patient need to go to  the operating room for some sort of other procedure to help with this or  was this something that could be dealt with medically? And so once we  had the results of the head CT and then the neck CT as well, it was felt  that the next appropriate step in his medical care would be to be  transitioned to the intensive care unit for them to take over care.

Jennifer Sprague ([21:52](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1312.02)):

Why was it that surgery was not an option to help Callan in this situation?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([21:58](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1318.14)):

Yeah.  The neurosurgery doctors were there to sort of help us make that  decision. There was no significant bleeding, for example, that would  need to be evacuated. There was nothing that they felt from a surgical  point of view would help alleviate the problems that we saw on the CT.

Jennifer Sprague ([22:10](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1330.77)):

Okay. And so was his care transferred to the ICU?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([22:15](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1335.45)):

That is correct.

Jennifer Sprague ([22:16](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1336.62)):

And once Callan was transferred to the intensive care unit, did your care for him end at that point?

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([22:22](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1342.98)):

Correct.

Jennifer Sprague ([22:23](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1343.52)):

I have nothing further. Thank you.

Judge William Sullivan ([22:32](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1352.01)):

Thank you. Mr. Reddington?

Kevin Reddington ([22:32](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1352.31)):

No questions. Thank you, Doctor.

Judge William Sullivan ([22:32](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1352.31)):

Thank you, Doctor. Please step down.

Dr. Andrew Capraro ([22:32](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1352.58)):

Thank you.

Court Officer 2 ([22:32](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1352.76)):

Watch your step, please.

Dr. David Casavant ([23:56](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1436.79)):

Sure.  I went to college out at Holy Cross in Worcester, and then to medical  school at Boston University. I did my initial pediatric residency at  Mass General Hospital, and then went back and did a fellowship in ICU  medicine at that hospital as well.

Jennifer Sprague ([24:13](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1453.92)):

And where do you currently work?

I currently work at Boston Children's Hospital in the Medical-Surgical ICU.

Jennifer Sprague ([24:19](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1459.71)):

18 years.

Jennifer Sprague ([24:24](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1464.3)):

And have you always worked in the ICU there?

Dr. David Casavant ([24:26](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1466.88)):

Yes.

Jennifer Sprague ([24:28](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1468.26)):

I  want to direct your attention to January 26th, 2023. At that point in  time, did you take over the care of a patient named Callan Clancy?

Dr. David Casavant ([24:38](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1478.97)):

I did.

Jennifer Sprague ([24:39](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1479.87)):

And prior to taking over the care of Callan Clancy, did you do anything to familiarize yourself with his treatment and care?

Dr. David Casavant ([24:48](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1488.81)):

Yes,  I did. One of my colleagues had taken care of him the day before in the  ICU, and I spoke with her, and so I was familiar with his case.

Jennifer Sprague ([24:58](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1498.35)):

Okay. And did you also review reports and testing that had been performed while he was at the hospital?

Dr. David Casavant ([25:05](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1505.61)):

Yes, I did.

Jennifer Sprague ([25:06](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1506.93)):

And  after reviewing the reports and the test results and speaking to your  colleague, can you tell us what you learned from all of that?

Dr. David Casavant ([25:18](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1518.63)):

Well, it seemed that Callan had had a severe neurologic injury based on both testing and the examination that I did at the time.

Jennifer Sprague ([25:30](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1530.33)):

And when you say a neurologic injury, is that a brain injury?

Dr. David Casavant ([25:33](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1533.39)):

It's a brain injury. Yes, it is. Yeah.

Jennifer Sprague ([25:35](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1535.19)):

And did you review specifically a head CT scan of Callan?

Dr. David Casavant ([25:42](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1542.75)):

Yes, I did.

Jennifer Sprague ([25:43](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1543.65)):

And what did the head CT scan show you or tell you about his condition?

Dr. David Casavant ([25:48](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1548.27)):

Well,  the head CT is a picture of the brain, which was done in the emergency  room when he was admitted to Boston Children's Hospital. And the CT scan  showed an enormous amount of swelling of the brain itself.

Jennifer Sprague ([26:07](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1567.83)):

And the swelling of the brain, is that medically significant?

Dr. David Casavant ([26:14](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1574.28)):

Very  much so. When you have an injury to the brain that is caused by anoxia,  which means a lack of oxygen, I described this with families that the  injury is very much like being out in the sun. You don't notice it at  the time, but swelling occurs over the course of time. And one of the  things with a lack of oxygen to the brain is the injury occurs and then  swelling starts to happen over the course of time. Typically peaking at  48 to 72 hours. When you see swelling that happens that early in a case,  as in Callan's, it means that there's been a very significant lack of  oxygen and therefore injury to the brain itself.

Jennifer Sprague ([27:05](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1625.52)):

So  for example, some types of injuries that would withhold oxygen from the  brain would be more mild and the swelling would occur over time. Is  that correct?

Dr. David Casavant ([27:18](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1638.06)):

That's exactly correct, yes.

Jennifer Sprague ([27:19](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1639.44)):

So if a severe injury happens that withholds oxygen from the brain, that's when you see that immediate swelling like Callan had?

Dr. David Casavant ([27:26](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1646.52)):

Very  much so. Sort of the comparison of a sunburn to a burn that you might  get from a stove. This was a stove. It's very, very profound.

Jennifer Sprague ([27:38](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1658.43)):

Did you perform a physical examination of Callan?

Dr. David Casavant ([27:40](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1660.98)):

I did.

Jennifer Sprague ([27:41](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1661.88)):

And what did you observe?

Dr. David Casavant ([27:43](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1663.89)):

Well,  one of the first things that I noticed as I approached him was that he  had a breathing tube in place. And I knew from my colleague that he was  not on any sedation, which is very difficult to tolerate having a  breathing tube through the vocal cords if you're not sedated. And he was  not sedated at the time. The next thing is when I approach a patient  such in their bed in the ICU, we always start with a quick introduction  that we're going to be coming near them in case they're asleep. And I  usually start with something along the lines of, "I'm Dr. Dave. I'm just  going to listen to your heart." And I'd usually address them by name to  see if they start to respond. And then I'll go ahead with the rest of  the exam, but that's just to sort of warn them that someone might be  touching them.

([28:34](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1714.38))
And  then I'll listen to heart, lungs, and then to move forward to see how  responsive they are. So if you get no response at all from the initial,  just using your voice, the next thing is to... And then from the exam,  if they don't move around, then you start to sort of think, "Well, let's  see how responsive they are." And so the next thing is sort of a gentle  touch often to the bottom of the foot to see, you stroke the bottom of  the foot, and you see if they pull away. And then you can move. If you  get no response from that, we'll do something that's a little bit more  noxious or painful where you can sort of pinch a little bit of skin to  see if they start to react or pushing on the fingernail and the nail bed  pressure, which is actually very noxious.

([29:19](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1759.65))
And  if you can see two types or several types of response, either no  response, or you can see a very specific response where they pull away  from that area. And that's a specific... Or you can see generalized  response, which are all sort of graded along those that as we're sort of  getting a sense for what their level of consciousness was.

Jennifer Sprague ([29:40](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1780.95)):

So if I could go over a few of those things that you just mentioned.

Dr. David Casavant ([29:43](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1783.62)):

Sorry.

Jennifer Sprague ([29:44](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1784.61)):

You  said that the first thing that you noticed that was significant was  that Callan had the breathing tube in place through the vocal cords  without any sedation. So without sedation, would having a breathing tube  in that place cause pain?

Dr. David Casavant ([30:03](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1803.21)):

Yes. No one would be able to tolerate it.

Jennifer Sprague ([30:06](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1806)):

And so Callan was able to tolerate it without any sedation?

Dr. David Casavant ([30:08](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1808.31)):

Yes.

Jennifer Sprague ([30:09](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1809.18)):

Would that indicate he wasn't feeling that pain at that time?

Dr. David Casavant ([30:12](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1812.33)):

Yes, exactly.

Jennifer Sprague ([30:14](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1814.28)):

And  you mentioned that you would address the patient by name and talk to  them. Was there any reaction from Callan when you did that?

Dr. David Casavant ([30:21](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1821.84)):

No, there was not.

Jennifer Sprague ([30:23](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1823.31)):

And then you mentioned you would rub maybe something along the foot. Was there any reaction?

Dr. David Casavant ([30:29](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1829.52)):

No, there was not.

Jennifer Sprague ([30:30](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1830.99)):

And did you do what you described in pinching the fingernail or pinching somewhere on Callan to see if he responded?

Dr. David Casavant ([30:37](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1837.95)):

Yes.

Jennifer Sprague ([30:38](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1838.67)):

And did he respond?

Dr. David Casavant ([30:39](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1839.48)):

No, he did not.

Jennifer Sprague ([30:41](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1841.67)):

And what did these interactions and tests on Callan tell you about his condition?

Dr. David Casavant ([30:50](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1850.43)):

This  told me that he was unresponsive. He was unresponsive to both things  that would cause discomfort and things that would cause pain.

Jennifer Sprague ([30:57](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1857.69)):

Did you also do anything in terms of looking at his pupils in any tests on his eyes?

Dr. David Casavant ([31:03](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1863.27)):

Yes.  So after all the other things, we look at the pupils. And so one of the  things is we have a, it's a reflex response. If a light is shown in an  eye, the pupil constricts, and what I noted about Callan was that his  pupils stayed widely open and did not constrict.

Jennifer Sprague ([31:26](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1886.25)):

And when someone's pupils stay open and wide when you shine a light in them, what does that indicate medically?

Dr. David Casavant ([31:33](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1893.21)):

Well,  the eyes are a direct extension of the brain, but that's why we focus a  lot on them as an area of investigation. And the lack of a response to  light indicates that there's some abnormality to that. And in this case,  it was a brain injury. You can also, there's medications that can keep  your eyes open if you've ever had an eye exam, but that was not the case  with Callan.

Jennifer Sprague ([31:59](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1919.79)):

And  just to be clear, when you're talking about brain injury, we're not  talking about some type of trauma that was inflicted on the brain.  You're talking about the lack of oxygen and blood flow causing an injury  to the brain, correct?

Dr. David Casavant ([32:12](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1932.42)):

That's exactly correct, yes.

Jennifer Sprague ([32:15](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1935.27)):

At that point in time, did you work with another doctor to do more in-depth testing regarding his neurological status?

Dr. David Casavant ([32:23](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1943.97)):

Yes.  So at Children's, we have multiple teams that come together for a  patient, for many patients, but for Callan, the neurology team was there  from the beginning. And in a case like this, when we see no response in  an unresponsive patient, we start to get our neurology team. And one of  the things we start to do is brain death testing to see if they could  be what we call brain-dead, meaning that the brain itself is not  functioning and that even though their heart is still beating.

Jennifer Sprague ([32:57](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1977.24)):

How is it that someone's heart can be beating if their brain is dead?

Dr. David Casavant ([33:02](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1982.61)):

It's a really good question. So...

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([33:00](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1980.51)):

... of their brain is dead.

David Casavant ([33:02](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=1982.31)):

It's  a really good question. So there's actually two parts to the nervous  system. The one is the part that we see the most in people when they  react, and they react to us, and they speak and they do things. The  other one is the autonomic or automatic system. And the automatic system  is largely in charge of the things like the heart if the heart has not  been injured. And so it was sort of continuing on.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([33:29](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2009.85)):

And  does that give you any indication, the fact that the heart was still  continuing on, about where the injuries were in the brain?

David Casavant ([33:38](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2018.31)):

Yes, it does actually. Yeah.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([33:40](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2020.11)):

Where would that be?

David Casavant ([33:40](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2020.77)):

Yeah.  So the large part of the brain that we sort of think of is when we see  the pictures of the brain, that's the cerebellum. And that's the biggest  thing in humans that is most developed. And that's in charge of our  consciousness, our reaction to things. Whereas the brain stem, which is  another part, and that's really part of the autonomic system. And that's  the same in almost all vertebrates and mammals. And it's much more  automatic. And so it tells me that it seemed the cerebellum, which is  that part that makes us sort of the human side of what we do, that's  much more sensitive to a lack of oxygen, to an anoxic injury. And it  told me that that was really where that injury was, was in that part of  the brain, much less so in the brain stem itself.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([34:33](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2073.27)):

So  would it be fair to say that the brainstem itself that controls those  automatic responses was still functioning somewhat, but the part of the  brain that made Callan, Callan was not functioning?

David Casavant ([34:44](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2084.79)):

That's exactly correct. Yeah.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([34:49](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2089.71)):

And could you describe for us the brain death testing?

David Casavant ([34:52](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2092.23)):

Sure.  So the brain death testing at Boston Children's it's a very strict  testing that includes some of the things that I just mentioned. But we  work with our... There's a checklist that we work on with our neurologic  colleagues. And we sort of go through each of what we would expect for  responses from someone. And we actually then work through different of  the nerve sets that we would expect them to respond to. We have it set  up so that there's an ICU physician is there, and then a neurologist is  there. And the neurologist and the neurology team, they're the ones who  perform the brain death testing. And then the test is then repeated  again 24 hours later, just to make sure that there's no piece of that  that is different or incongruous with a diagnosis of brain death.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([35:52](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2152.23)):

And  while the neurologist is doing this testing, are you there present  watching the testing and seeing the results as it happens?

David Casavant ([35:58](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2158.38)):

Absolutely.  Yeah. So we're sort of committed to being there for the whole time. And  the brain death testing itself goes through some really basic things.  Again, as I described earlier, the things that would be painful and do  they pull away or not? The eyes, looking at the reflexes of the eyes.  There's also other reflexes too. Things like if you stroke the cornea,  which is the front of the eye, people blink. So the blink reflex, cough,  gag. There's also a reflex where they actually put cold water into an  ear and that gives people a disequilibrium. And so those are all the  things that we think about. And then also too, there's some breathing  components to that as well.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([36:44](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2204.88)):

Is one of those breathing components an apnea test?

David Casavant ([36:47](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2207.58)):

Yes, it is. Yeah.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([36:48](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2208.69)):

And what is an apnea test?

David Casavant ([36:50](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2210.25)):

So apnea is a word that just describes a lack of breathing.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([36:59](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2219.25)):

And how do you test that?

David Casavant ([37:02](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2222.79)):

So  in a patient such as Callan, what we do, and in any patient when we're  doing brain death testing, we actually disconnect them from the  ventilator. And the reason that we do that is we want to see if that  autonomic system in the brain will start to react as the carbon dioxide  starts to increase. So our body makes carbon dioxide all the time. And  the way we get rid of it is by exhaling. And where we breathe in fresh  oxygen and then we breathe out CO2. And what we do is we disconnect from  the ventilator, knowing that their carbon dioxide will start to rise.  And the normal response for that autonomic part of the brain would be to  sort of increase breathing. That's why we breathe when we sleep,  because as we go to sleep, the conscious part of our brain stops, but  the autonomic part kicks in, and then we breathe right through our sleep  because that carbon dioxide rises.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([38:08](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2288.49)):

So  in a patient who has brain death, would it be accurate to state that as  the carbon dioxide level increases, the automatic response would be to  take a breath to get more oxygen, but a patient who's brain-dead won't  do that?

David Casavant ([38:22](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2302.59)):

That's correct.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([38:23](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2303.94)):

And did you do all of these tests or did the neurologist do all these tests on Callan while you observed?

David Casavant ([38:31](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2311.56)):

Yes, they did. Yeah.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([38:32](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2312.7)):

And did he have any response to any of the physical tests where you were looking for a reflex or a reaction?

David Casavant ([38:38](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2318.64)):

No, he did not. No response to any.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([38:39](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2319.81)):

And what was his reaction or results on the apnea test?

David Casavant ([38:43](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2323.68)):

So  the apnea test, again, we do all the brain testing one day, and then we  wait at least 24 hours for the second one. And what we expect with the  carbon dioxide is that it will increase. We have it written in our... So  it's all in writing for our protocol, that it will increase by 20 or  get to a level of 60. A normal CO2 would be around 40. And the first  time we did that on the first day, it actually went to 59.7, not 60. And  again, it was in our protocol as it has to get to 60 or increase by 20.  And I actually talked to my colleagues, including neurology. I said, "I  know this is very close. It's 59.7, but it's not 60." I repeated that  piece of the test later on that day to make sure that it was valid. And  as I say, I checked with everyone to make sure that I was doing the  right thing.

([39:48](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2388.36))
And  they all agreed that we should just... And the second time it went to  87. So everything else was the same. And we made sure that there's  things that we look at too to make sure that there's not on any  medications, that there's no changes in his temperature, there's no  abnormalities of the salts and the blood, what we call the electrolytes.  So all those conditions were the exact same. And I repeated that later  on in the day and it did come back positive.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([40:14](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2414.43)):

Why is it so important that you meet that specific criteria? Why is three tenths of a point so important?

David Casavant ([40:20](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2420.91)):

Well, I wanted to make sure that there was no question about the data that we were gathering at the time.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([40:29](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2429.82)):

And you said that this testing is again repeated 24 hours later, correct?

David Casavant ([40:33](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2433.87)):

Yes, it is.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([40:34](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2434.29)):

Why is that?

David Casavant ([40:35](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2435.31)):

Well,  that's just to make sure that if there were any lingering effects of  medications, if there was anything that else might've changed in the  patient's level of alertness or level of response, to make sure that it  still meets all those requirements 24 hours later.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([40:52](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2452.65)):

And is it because you want to be sure that there's brain death before you remove life support?

David Casavant ([40:58](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2458.62)):

Yes. And again, it's too important. It's too important, a discussion and a gathering of data to not be very exact about it.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([41:08](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2468.73)):

And  so when you observe the testing and then the following day, on Callan,  were the results the same in terms of no reaction and the carbon dioxide  increasing?

David Casavant ([41:20](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2480.34)):

Yes, they were.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([41:21](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2481.75)):

And at that point in time, did he meet brain death criteria?

David Casavant ([41:27](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2487.81)):

Yeah, he did. So we had determined that he had met death by neurologic criteria.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([41:38](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2498.28)):

And at that point in time, was a decision made to stop life support?

David Casavant ([41:46](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2506.17)):

Yes. Yes, it was.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([41:47](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2507.19)):

And that's done in consultation with the family, his father, correct?

David Casavant ([41:50](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2510.58)):

Yes, that's true.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([41:51](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2511.96)):

Okay. And was life support ended for Callan Clancy on January 27th, 2023?

David Casavant ([42:01](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2521.11)):

It  was. And one of the things is when you determine that someone has met  criteria for neurologic brain death, although we know that their brain  is not functioning at that point, there's often a period of time where  we don't have to... His heart is still beating, so we don't have to  remove the endotracheal tube at that time. And we often will give  families, once it's been confirmed that the brain is no longer  functioning, we often give families a period of time to be able to  process it and a window of time. Sometimes his relatives will need to  travel and things like that. And so we'll often then choose a time later  on to accommodate the rest of life, knowing that they're stable at the  time to when we would actually remove the tube. And that would be the  declaration of death.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([43:00](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2580.06)):

And  was that done in this case? Family was given an opportunity to see him  and then life support was ended and then his heart stopped.

David Casavant ([43:07](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2587.98)):

Yes, that's exactly.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([43:09](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2589.45)):

And he was declared deceased?

David Casavant ([43:10](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2590.65)):

Yes.

Attorney Shanan Buckingham ([43:11](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE3MWNiZTFhNjZmN2RkZjg4YTQ3NzkxM3I4RlVZNTZCWVVT/o/VEMwMTg3Mjg4MjU3?ts=2591.73)):

Thank you.
